         │                             └──────────────────┘            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.2 Configuring the Kubernetes Plugin
         - Installing @backstage/plugin-kubernetes and backend
         - Configuring app-config.yaml for K8s clusters
         - Service Account and RBAC for Backstage
         - Cluster authentication: ServiceAccount token, OIDC
         [CODE BLOCK: Complete K8s plugin configuration]

         [KUBERNETES PLUGIN CONFIGURATION]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                           │
         │  kubernetes:                                                 │
         │    serviceLocatorMethod:                                     │
         │      type: 'multiTenant'                                     │
         │    clusterLocatorMethods:                                    │
         │      - type: 'config'                                        │
         │        clusters:                                             │
         │          - name: 'easypj-cluster'                            │
         │            url: 'https://k8s-api.easypj.com'                 │
         │            authProvider: 'serviceAccount'                    │
         │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_SA_TOKEN}              │
         │            skipTLSVerify: false                              │
         │            dashboardUrl: 'https://k8s-dash.easypj.com'       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] 💡 'config' is only ONE of several valid
         clusterLocatorMethods types. The official Kubernetes plugin docs
         also list 'catalog' (derive clusters from Catalog entities instead
         of static config), 'gke' (auto-discover Google Kubernetes Engine
         clusters), and 'localKubectlProxy' (use your local kubectl proxy —
         handy for local development). easypj's example above uses the
         static 'config' method, which is the right choice for a small,
         fixed set of clusters.

    10.3 Linking K8s Resources to Catalog Entities
         - backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation
         - backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace annotation
         - backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector annotation
         - How Backstage matches K8s resources to entities
         [K8s ENTITY-LINKING ANNOTATIONS — Complete]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  annotations:                                                                │
         │    backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost              # match Deployment name  │
         │    backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: production     # override namespace    │
         │    backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector:            # OR match by label    │
         │      'app=jobpost,env=production'                                            │
         │                                                                              │
         │  🔑 Use kubernetes-id for exact name matches (most common).                  │
         │     Use kubernetes-label-selector when one entity maps to MULTIPLE           │
         │     K8s objects that share a label but not an exact name.                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INFO BOX] 🔑 One More Official Annotation — backstage.io/
         kubernetes-cluster lets an entity pin itself to ONE specific,
         named cluster. It is used together with the singleTenant
         serviceLocatorMethod (see 10.2), where Backstage must be told
         exactly which configured cluster a given entity's resources live
         in, rather than searching every configured cluster.

    10.4 Viewing K8s Resources in Backstage
         - Entity page: Kubernetes tab
         - Pod status, logs, events
         - Deployment details, replica sets
         - Service endpoints, ingress URLs
         [KUBERNETES TAB — What You'll See on the Entity Page]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☸️ KUBERNETES                                                        │
         │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  Deployment: jobpost          🟢 3/3 pods Running               │  │
         │  │  ReplicaSet: jobpost-7d9f8    🟢 Current                        │  │
         │  │  Service:    jobpost-svc      ClusterIP · :8000                 │  │
         │  │  Ingress:    jobpost.easypj.com  🔒 TLS active                  │  │
         │  │  Pod events: 2 min ago — Started container jobpost              │  │
         │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    10.5 Deploying easypj to Kubernetes (via Backstage)
         - Dockerfile for easypj/jobpost Django app
         - Kubernetes manifests: Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret
         - Registering the deployment in Backstage Catalog
         - Viewing deployment status in Backstage
         [COMPLETE WALKTHROUGH with all YAML files]

         [DOCKERFILE for easypj/jobpost]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Dockerfile for easypj/jobpost Django Application          │
         │                                                              │
         │  FROM python:3.11-slim                                       │
         │                                                              │
         │  ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1                               │
         │  ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1                                      │
         │                                                              │
         │  WORKDIR /app                                                │
         │                                                              │
         │  RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y \                  │
         │      gcc libpq-dev && \                                     │
         │      rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  COPY requirements.txt .                                     │
         │  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt          │
         │                                                              │
         │  COPY . .                                                    │
         │                                                              │
         │  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput                │
         │                                                              │
         │  EXPOSE 8000                                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │  CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", \               │
         │       "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", \                           │
         │       "--workers", "3"]                                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [UNDER THE HOOD 🔧]
         clusterLocatorMethods supports more than the static 'config' type
         easypj uses: 'catalog' derives clusters from Catalog entities
         instead of static config, 'gke' auto-discovers Google Kubernetes
         Engine clusters, and 'localKubectlProxy' uses your local kubectl
         proxy for development. Separately, the backstage.io/kubernetes-
         cluster annotation lets one entity pin itself to exactly one named
         cluster — used together with the singleTenant serviceLocatorMethod
         when Backstage must be told which configured cluster an entity's
         resources live in, rather than searching every configured cluster.

    [KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑]
         🔑 The K8s plugin auto-discovers Deployments, Pods, Services and
            Ingresses and renders them on the entity page.
         🔑 Clusters are configured under kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods —
            config, catalog, gke or localKubectlProxy.
         🔑 kubernetes-id matches by exact name; kubernetes-label-selector
            matches when one entity maps to multiple K8s objects sharing a
            label.
         🔑 kubernetes-cluster pins an entity to one named cluster, paired
            with the singleTenant serviceLocatorMethod.
         🔑 The entity page's Kubernetes tab shows live pod/replica/service/
            ingress status, not a static snapshot.
         🔑 Deploying easypj to Kubernetes follows the same Dockerfile ->
            manifests -> register-in-Catalog flow as any other Component.

    [EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️]
         ⚠️ Forgetting the ServiceAccount/RBAC Backstage itself needs to
            query the cluster.
         ⚠️ Using kubernetes-id when the target is actually multiple objects
            sharing a label — that calls for kubernetes-label-selector
            instead.
         ⚠️ Assuming 'config' is the only clusterLocatorMethods type —
            catalog, gke and localKubectlProxy also exist.
         ⚠️ Skipping the kubernetes-namespace override and having Backstage
            look in the wrong namespace.

    [SPEED TIPS ⚡]
         ⚡ Reach for kubernetes-label-selector as soon as one entity maps to
            more than one K8s object.
         ⚡ Use localKubectlProxy locally instead of standing up a real
            ServiceAccount while developing.
         ⚡ Check the entity's Kubernetes tab before reaching for kubectl —
            it's often faster for a quick status check.

    [COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER 🔑]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # app-config.yaml                                                       │
         │  kubernetes:                                                             │
         │    serviceLocatorMethod: { type: 'multiTenant' }                         │
         │    clusterLocatorMethods:                                                │
         │      - type: 'config'   # or: catalog | gke | localKubectlProxy          │
         │        clusters: [{ name, url, authProvider, serviceAccountToken }]      │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # catalog-info.yaml annotations                                         │
         │  backstage.io/kubernetes-id: jobpost              # exact name match     │
         │  backstage.io/kubernetes-namespace: production    # override namespace   │
         │  backstage.io/kubernetes-label-selector: 'app=jobpost,env=production'    │
         │  backstage.io/kubernetes-cluster: easypj-cluster   # pin to ONE cluster  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [PRACTICE DRILL 🧪]
         🧪 In 20 minutes:
         1. Configure a static cluster under kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods
            for easypj-cluster (6 min).
         2. Add kubernetes-id: jobpost to jobpost's catalog-info.yaml
            (3 min).
         3. Confirm the Kubernetes tab shows 3/3 pods running (3 min).
         4. Write the Dockerfile CMD line that serves easypj.wsgi with
            gunicorn (3 min).
         5. Note which annotation you'd add if jobpost ran across 3
            differently-named Deployments sharing one label (5 min).

    10.6 CBA Exam: Chapter 10 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: K8s plugin, configuration, annotations, resource linking

    [VISUAL SUMMARY — Chapter 10: "From YAML to a live Kubernetes dashboard"]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ☸️ easypj-cluster                    📦 jobpost Component (Catalog)  │
         │    Deployment: jobpost (3 pods)  ──▶    Kubernetes tab:               │
         │    Service:    jobpost-svc               • 3/3 pods Running           │
         │    Ingress:    jobpost.easypj.com         • Service, Ingress, events  │
         │                                                                       │
         │    matched via backstage.io/kubernetes-id (10.3) after the plugin     │
         │    is configured under kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods (10.2)        │
         │                                                                       │
         │    Dockerfile ──▶ K8s manifests ──▶ register in Catalog (10.5)        │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    .vs-prose — In words: once the Kubernetes plugin is configured (10.2)
    and jobpost's catalog-info.yaml carries a kubernetes-id annotation
    (10.3), the entity page's Kubernetes tab shows the real state of the
    easypj-cluster Deployment — pod count, service, ingress and recent
    events (10.4) — instead of documentation someone has to keep updated by
    hand. The Dockerfile-to-manifests-to-Catalog flow in 10.5 is the same
    path every Component takes to production, just walked once, end to end,
    for jobpost.


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
  🔑 PART 3 — MUST-REMEMBER
   • The Catalog is Backstage's single source of truth; nothing is real until
     it's ingested via a Location (manual) or an EntityProvider (automated).
   • Ingestion pipeline: Location/Provider -> Processor -> Catalog DB -> API/UI.
   • 9 entity kinds exist (Component, API, System, Domain, Resource, User,
     Group, Location, Template); owner is MANDATORY on Component, API,
     System, Resource and Domain.
   • Every catalog-info.yaml needs exactly 4 top-level fields: apiVersion,
     kind, metadata, spec — spec fields differ by kind.
   • Every YAML relationship field (owner, system, providesApis, dependsOn...)
     has a paired auto-generated graph relation name (e.g. dependsOn/
     dependencyOf) — the exam can ask for either.
   • A missing entity is debugged in a strict order: is the Location/Provider
     configured? -> reachable? -> valid YAML? -> has ingestion run? -> logs.
   • The Kubernetes plugin turns a static entity into a live operational view
     via the kubernetes-id / kubernetes-label-selector annotations.

  [PART 3 VISUAL SUMMARY — from a GitHub repo to a live entity page]
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GitHub repo (catalog-info.yaml)                                 │
  │            │                                                     │
  │            ▼                                                     │
  │  Location / EntityProvider  ──▶  Processor  ──▶  📦 Catalog DB   │
  │    (Ch7.3, 7.6)                   (Ch7.2)          (PostgreSQL)  │
  │                                                          │       │
  │                                                          ▼       │
  │  Entity page: Overview · API · Relations · ☸️ Kubernetes tab     │
  │    (built from Ch8 kinds + Ch9 YAML fields + Ch10 K8s link)      │
  │                                                          │       │
  │                                                          ▼       │
  │                 ☸️ LIVE easypj-cluster (real pods/services)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  .vs-prose — In words: a catalog-info.yaml sitting in a GitHub repo is
  worthless until a Location or an automated EntityProvider tells the Catalog
  it exists (Ch7). Once found, a Processor transforms and validates it before
  it lands in the Catalog's PostgreSQL database (Ch7, Ch9.6). From there it
  is queryable through the API and rendered as an entity page built from the
  kind, relationships and spec fields you wrote (Ch8-9). Finally, if that
  entity's annotations point at a real Kubernetes cluster, the entity page's
  Kubernetes tab stops being documentation and starts being a live dashboard
  of the actual running Deployment (Ch10) — the same jobpost you first ran
  locally in Part 2 is now discoverable, describable and observable by the
  whole organization.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────


PART 4: SOFTWARE TEMPLATES — SCAFFOLDING MADE EASY                     🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 4 INTRO]
  Part 3 gave easypj a Software Catalog — a single source of truth that
  auto-discovers services, tracks ownership, and shows what is running where.
  But the Catalog only reflects what already exists. Part 4 answers the next
  question every growing engineering org hits: how do NEW services get INTO
  the Catalog in the first place? Today, scaffolding a new easypj microservice
  by hand — clone a similar repo, delete what you don't need, rename
  everything, wire up CI/CD, create the GitHub repo, push, register it — burns
  about 2 hours per service. Software Templates turn that into a 2-minute form.

  Part 4 builds the templating system layer by layer. Chapter 11 introduces
  what a Software Template actually is and the anatomy of a template.yaml file
  (metadata, parameters, steps). Chapter 12 goes one level deeper: the full
  catalog of built-in actions, the skeleton/ directory fetch:template actually
  copies, writing your own custom action, and testing/debugging a template
  before you ship it. Chapter 13 makes it all concrete with the 4 real
  templates the easypj platform team maintains: a Django microservice, a React
  frontend, a database migration job, and a Terraform infrastructure module.

  🧠 ANALOGY. Think of a Software Template as a cookie-cutter factory, not a
  hand-sculptor. Instead of shaping each new cookie (service) from raw dough
  every time — measuring, cutting, decorating by hand — you press one stamped
  shape (template.yaml) into the dough and a fully-formed cookie pops out:
  Dockerfile, CI pipeline, catalog-info.yaml and all, already baked in. Change
  the cutter once, and every future cookie comes out consistent, correct, and
  fast — that consistency is exactly what makes a growing easypj engineering
  org scale without every new service reinventing its own scaffolding.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 4 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Ch11                  Ch12                  Ch13            │
  │    WHAT a Template is       HOW it runs        APPLIED to easypj      │
  │                                                                       │
  │      template.yaml,        fetch/publish/      4 real templates,      │
  │  parameters (the form)    register, custom   registered in Catalog    │
  │                          actions, debugging                           │
  │                                                                       │
  │             │                     │                     │             │
  │             └─────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘             │
  │                                   ▼                                   │
  │                                                                       │
  │  Every easypj template shares ONE shape, learned once and reused 4x:  │
  │      fetch:template  ──▶  publish:github  ──▶  catalog:register       │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Read the map as a sentence: Chapter 11 teaches WHAT a template is, Chapter
  12 teaches HOW its actions actually run (and what to do when the built-ins
  aren't enough), and Chapter 13 APPLIES both to the 4 templates easypj ships
  today. All 3 chapters converge on the same fetch → publish → register shape.

  🧭 DECISION GUIDE — where do I start?
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ If you...                              │ Then...                                 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ have never built a template before     │ start at 11.1, read the template.yaml   │
  │                                        │ anatomy box in 11.2 slowly              │
  │ already write YAML/config forms daily  │ skim 11.1-11.3, focus on 11.4 actions   │
  │                                        │ and all of Chapter 12                   │
  │ need to write a custom action          │ jump straight to 12.3                   │
  │ just need copy-paste templates for     │ go straight to Chapter 13 — all 4       │
  │ easypj                                 │ templates are complete there            │
  │ are revising for the CBA exam          │ read every chapter Key Takeaways        │
  │                                        │ and Gotchas block                       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 4 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION — read before the chapters]
  By the end of this part you will have built (on paper) all 4 real easypj
  Software Templates — a Django microservice generator, a React frontend
  generator, a database migration job generator, and a Terraform
  infrastructure generator — understood the shared fetch → publish:github →
  catalog:register shape every one of them runs, registered all 4 into the
  Catalog via catalog.locations in app-config.yaml, and know when (rarely) you
  need to write a custom scaffolder action of your own.

Chapter 11: Software Templates — Scaffolding New Projects 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~50min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
    Every time a new microservice, frontend, or migration job needs to exist
    for easypj, someone used to clone the jobpost repo, delete half of it,
    rename everything, and wire up CI/CD by hand — roughly 2 hours of manual
    work per new service, every single time. This chapter introduces Software
    Templates, the mechanism behind the "Create" button that turns that
    2-hour manual process into a 2-minute form fill.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ explain what a Software Template is and why it replaces manual
       copy-paste scaffolding;
    ✅ describe the anatomy of a template.yaml file (metadata, spec.type,
       parameters, steps);
    ✅ list the built-in parameter types and UI fields (OwnerPicker,
       RepoUrlPicker, EntityPicker, enum dropdowns);
    ✅ trace what happens end-to-end when a developer clicks "Create"
       (fetch → publish → register);
    ✅ identify the built-in scaffolder actions used in a typical template
       (fetch:template, publish:github, catalog:register);
    ✅ follow how a newly scaffolded easypj service flows from repo creation
       through CI/CD to a running Kubernetes deployment.

    CBA RELEVANCE: Software Templates fall under the Customizing domain
    (🎯 32% of the CBA exam) — the single largest weighted domain, so
    template.yaml anatomy, parameters, and action wiring are high-yield
    exam material.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    11.1 What are Software Templates? → 11.2 Template Anatomy (template.yaml)
    → 11.3 Template Parameters (the user input form) → 11.4 Template Actions
    (what happens when you click "Create") → 11.5 CBA Exam: Chapter 11
    Practice Questions.

    11.1 What are Software Templates?
         - The "Create" button that scaffolds new projects
         - Replaces: "Copy-paste from another repo + manually edit"
         - Template = Blueprint + Actions + Parameters
         - Examples: New microservice, new Django app, new React component
         [BEFORE/AFTER DIAGRAM: Manual scaffolding vs Templates]

         [TEMPLATES — BEFORE vs AFTER]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ WITHOUT TEMPLATES                                       │
         │  1. Find a similar repo... 20 min                            │
         │  2. Clone it... 5 min                                        │
         │  3. Delete what you don't need... 15 min                     │
         │  4. Rename everything... 30 min                              │
         │  5. Update configs... 20 min                                 │
         │  6. Create repo in GitHub... 5 min                           │
         │  7. Push code... 5 min                                       │
         │  8. Set up CI/CD... 30 min                                   │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                   │
         │  TOTAL: ~2 HOURS                                             │
         │                                                              │
         │  ✅ WITH BACKSTAGE TEMPLATES                                │
         │  1. Click "Create" → Choose Template → Fill Form → Done     │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                   │
         │  TOTAL: ~2 MINUTES (everything automated!)                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.2 Template Anatomy — template.yaml
         - apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3
         - kind: Template
         - metadata: name, title, description, tags
         - spec:
           - type: service | website | library | documentation
           - parameters: User input form fields
           - steps: Actions to execute (fetch, run, publish, etc.)
         [COMPLETE ANATOMY of a template.yaml file]

         [TEMPLATE ANATOMY — Complete template.yaml]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3                 │
         │  kind: Template                                              │
         │  metadata:                                                   │
         │    name: django-service                                      │
         │    title: Django Microservice                                │
         │    description: Scaffold a new Django service for easypj     │
         │    tags: [django, python, microservice, recommended]         │
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    owner: team-platform                                      │
         │    type: service                                             │
         │                                                              │
         │    # ── USER INPUTS ──                                       │
         │    parameters:                                               │
         │      - title: Service Details                                │
         │        properties:                                           │
         │          name:                                               │
         │            title: Service Name                               │
         │            type: string                                      │
         │            pattern: '^[a-z0-9-]+$'                          │
         │          description:                                        │
         │            title: Description                                │
         │            type: string                                      │
         │          owner:                                              │
         │            title: Owner                                      │
         │            type: string                                      │
         │            ui:field: OwnerPicker                             │
         │                                                              │
         │    # ── ACTIONS TO EXECUTE ──                                │
         │    steps:                                                    │
         │      - id: fetch-template                                    │
         │        name: Fetch Template                                  │
         │        action: fetch:template                                │
         │        input:                                                │
         │          url: ./template                                     │
         │                                                              │
         │      - id: publish                                           │
         │        name: Publish to GitHub                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                │
         │        input:                                                │
         │          repoUrl: github.com?owner=${{ parameters.owner }}   │
         │                             &repo=${{ parameters.name }}    │
         │          defaultBranch: main                                 │
         │                                                              │
         │      - id: register                                          │
         │        name: Register in Catalog                             │
         │        action: catalog:register                              │
         │        input:                                                │
         │          repoContentsUrl: ${{ steps.publish.output.repoUrl }}│
         │          catalogInfoPath: /catalog-info.yaml                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.3 Template Parameters — The User Input Form
         - Parameter types: string, number, boolean, array, object
         - UI fields: OwnerPicker, RepoUrlPicker, EntityPicker
         - Validation: pattern, minLength, maxLength, enum
         - Conditional fields: if/then/else logic
         - Dynamic options: enum from API
         [PARAMETER TYPES — Complete Reference]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  TYPE       UI FIELD              EXAMPLE                               │
         │  ---------  --------------------  -----------------------------------   │
         │  string      (default text box)     name: { type: string }              │
         │  string      OwnerPicker            owner: { type: string, ui:field:    │
         │                                     OwnerPicker }                       │
         │  string      RepoUrlPicker          repoUrl: { type: string, ui:field:  │
         │                                     RepoUrlPicker }                     │
         │  string      EntityPicker           system: { ui:field: EntityPicker }  │
         │  boolean     (checkbox)              includeDocker: { type: boolean }   │
         │  number       (number input)          replicas: { type: number }        │
         │  array        (multi-select)          tags: { type: array, items:       │
         │                                     { type: string } }                  │
         │  string       enum (dropdown)         pythonVersion: { enum:            │
         │                                     ['3.10','3.11','3.12'] }            │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    11.4 Template Actions — What Happens When You Click "Create"
         - fetch:template — Copy template files
         - fetch:plain — Copy plain files from URL
         - publish:github — Create GitHub repository
         - publish:gitlab — Create GitLab repository
         - catalog:register — Register in Backstage Catalog
         - fetch:cookiecutter — Use Cookiecutter templates
         - debug:wait — Wait (for testing)
         - Custom actions — Build your own!
         [TABLE: All built-in actions with descriptions]

         [TEMPLATE EXECUTION FLOW]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  🔄 TEMPLATE EXECUTION FLOW                                  │
         │                                                              │
         │  👤 User fills form                                          │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  📝 Parameters collected                                     │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  ⬇️ Step 1: fetch:template                                   │
         │     Copy skeleton files (Django boilerplate)                 │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  🔧 Step 2: (optional custom actions)                        │
         │     Run cookiecutter, replace placeholders                   │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  🚀 Step 3: publish:github                                   │
         │     Create repo, push code, set up branches                  │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  📦 Step 4: catalog:register                                 │
         │     Register new entity in Backstage Catalog                 │
         │      │                                                       │
         │      ▼                                                       │
         │  ✅ Done! New service is live in Catalog!                    │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
    When a developer clicks Create, the scaffolder spins up a temporary
    workspace for that run. The first step (typically fetch:template) writes
    files into that workspace and replaces every {{ values.X }} placeholder
    with the submitted parameter. Each later step's OUTPUT becomes available
    to every subsequent step as ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — this is how
    the publish step's repoUrl can flow into the register step's
    repoContentsUrl without either action knowing the other exists. This
    output-chaining is exactly why a template can compose fetch → publish →
    register into one pipeline instead of three unrelated manual scripts.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
     🔑 A Software Template = template.yaml (the blueprint) + parameters
        (the form) + steps (the actions).
     🔑 template.yaml always declares apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/
        v1beta3 and kind: Template.
     🔑 spec.type (service | website | library | documentation) classifies
        what kind of thing gets scaffolded.
     🔑 Parameters map directly onto form fields the developer fills in the
        Backstage UI; ui:field pickers like OwnerPicker/RepoUrlPicker add
        validation and catalog lookups on top of a plain text box.
     🔑 The near-universal 3-step shape is fetch → publish → register: copy
        the skeleton, push it to GitHub, register the result in the Catalog.
     🔑 Manual scaffolding of a new easypj service took ~2 hours by hand; a
        template reduces this to ~2 minutes end to end.
     🔑 Once registered, the new service is immediately visible in the
        Catalog, picked up by the same ingestion pipeline (Part 3, 7.2) as
        every other entity.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
     ⚠️ Forgetting rules: [{allow: [Template]}] on a type: file
        catalog.locations entry — without it Backstage won't recognize the
        file as a Template kind.
     ⚠️ Confusing spec.type (a free-form classification: service/website/
        library/documentation) with kind: Template (the entity kind itself)
        — they answer two different questions.
     ⚠️ Leaving a required parameter out of `required: [...]` — the form then
        lets a developer submit it empty, breaking a later step that assumes
        it's filled.
     ⚠️ Assuming fetch:template creates a GitHub repository — it only copies
        files into a local/temp workspace; publish:github is a separate,
        later step.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
     ⚡ Reuse the standard fetch → publish → register 3-step shape as your
        starting skeleton for any new template — usually only the skeleton/
        contents need to change.
     ⚡ Use ui:field: OwnerPicker/EntityPicker instead of a free-text string
        wherever the answer must resolve to an existing catalog entity — it
        prevents typos that would otherwise break catalog:register.
     ⚡ Test a new template.yaml locally with `yarn dev` before publishing it
        org-wide.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 11 — COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER                                   │
         │                                                                             │
         │  $ mkdir -p templates/django-service/skeleton                               │
         │                                                                             │
         │  # Register a template as a catalog location (app-config.yaml)              │
         │  catalog:                                                                   │
         │    locations:                                                               │
         │      - type: file                                                           │
         │        target: ./templates/django-service/template.yaml                     │
         │        rules:                                                               │
         │          - allow: [Template]                                                │
         │                                                                             │
         │  $ yarn dev   # then: click CREATE -> choose template -> fill form -> Done  │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Chapter 12: Template Actions & Customization 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~55min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
    Chapter 11 showed the 3-action skeleton (fetch → publish → register) that
    covers most easypj templates. But real organizations eventually need
    something the built-ins don't do — post to Slack when a service is
    created, call an internal ticketing API, enforce an org-specific naming
    check. Chapter 12 goes one level deeper: the complete built-in action
    catalog, the skeleton/ directory that fetch:template actually copies, and
    how to write and debug your own custom action when nothing built-in fits.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ list the complete built-in scaffolder action catalog (fetch:*,
       publish:*, catalog:*, fs:delete, roadiehq:utils:fs:replace) and each
       one's key inputs/outputs;
    ✅ trace how a step's output becomes available to a later step via
       ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }};
    ✅ read a template's spec.output.links/text and explain what a user
       actually sees after a scaffolder run finishes;
    ✅ describe the skeleton/ directory layout and how {{ values.X }}
       placeholders get replaced during fetch:template;
    ✅ write a minimal custom action with createTemplateAction, including its
       input schema and handler;
    ✅ decide when a custom action is actually needed versus when a built-in
       action already covers the job;
    ✅ run a template locally and diagnose the 3 most common template errors.

    CBA RELEVANCE: Template Actions still lands in the Customizing domain
    (🎯 32%) — expect exam tasks that ask you to add or fix a step, wire an
    output reference, or diagnose why a placeholder was never replaced.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    12.1 Built-in Actions (Complete Reference) → 12.2 The Template Skeleton
    (./template Directory) → 12.3 Custom Template Actions → 12.4 Template
    Testing & Debugging → 12.5 CBA Exam: Chapter 12 Practice Questions.

    12.1 Built-in Actions — Complete Reference
         - fetch:template — Template from local directory
         - fetch:plain — Copy files from URL
         - fetch:cookiecutter — Cookiecutter templates
         - publish:github — Create GitHub repo
         - publish:gitlab — Create GitLab repo
         - publish:bitbucket — Create Bitbucket repo
         - publish:azure — Create Azure DevOps repo
         - catalog:register — Register entity
         - catalog:write — Write catalog entity
         - fs:delete — Delete files during template run
         - roadiehq:utils:fs:replace — Find and replace
         [ALL BUILT-IN ACTIONS — Inputs, Outputs, Examples]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ACTION                 KEY INPUTS              OUTPUT                                         │
         │  ---------------------  ----------------------  ----------------------                         │
         │  fetch:template          url, values              (files written to disk)                      │
         │  fetch:plain              url, targetPath           (files written to disk)                    │
         │  fetch:cookiecutter        url, values, extract       (files written to disk)                  │
         │  publish:github              repoUrl, defaultBranch    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl              │
         │  publish:gitlab                repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl           │
         │  publish:bitbucket                repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl        │
         │  publish:azure                       repoUrl                    remoteUrl, repoContentsUrl     │
         │  catalog:register                       repoContentsUrl,          entityRef, catalogInfoUrl    │
         │                                        catalogInfoPath                                         │
         │  catalog:write                            entity                     (writes YAML to disk)     │
         │  fs:delete                                  files                      (removes files)         │
         │  roadiehq:utils:fs:replace                   files, replace             (edits file contents)  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - How actions actually chain together at run time
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  SCAFFOLDER ACTION EXECUTION FLOW — django-service Template                      │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  Developer fills out the form (name=jobpost-worker, owner=team-platform)         │
         │                              │                                                   │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "fetch"    action: fetch:template                                          │
         │                  reads ./skeleton, replaces {{ values.name }} -> jobpost-worker  │
         │                  OUTPUT: files written to a temp workspace                       │
         │                              │  (workspace path passed to the next step)         │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "publish"  action: publish:github                                          │
         │                  pushes the temp workspace to a new GitHub repo                  │
         │                  OUTPUT: repoContentsUrl, remoteUrl                              │
         │                              │  (repoContentsUrl passed to the next step)        │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  STEP "register" action: catalog:register                                        │
         │                  reads catalog-info.yaml from repoContentsUrl, registers it      │
         │                  OUTPUT: entityRef, catalogInfoUrl                               │
         │                              │                                                   │
         │                              ▼                                                   │
         │  Scaffolder UI shows: "Open in Catalog" -> jobpost-worker Component page         │
         │                                                                                  │
         │  KEY RULE: each step's OUTPUT becomes available to every later step as           │
         │  {{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — this is how publish knows nothing about       │
         │  fetch, yet register can still reach back to what publish produced.              │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - spec.output — what the user sees after the run finishes
         [TEMPLATE OUTPUT — Links & Text Shown to the User]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  spec:                                                       │
         │    steps: [...]                                              │
         │    output:                                                   │
         │      links:                                                  │
         │        - title: Open in catalog                              │
         │          url: ${{ steps.register.output.entityRef }}         │
         │        - title: Repository                                   │
         │          url: ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}          │
         │      text:                                                   │
         │        - title: Next Steps                                   │
         │          content: |                                          │
         │            Your service is live at                           │
         │            ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}             │
         │                                                              │
         │  RULE: output.links/output.text is what renders on the       │
         │  scaffolder "Task" completion page — without it the user     │
         │  only sees raw step logs, no clickable link to their repo.   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.2 The Template Skeleton — ./template Directory
         - Directory structure for template files
         - Using ${{ values.name }} for placeholders
         - Template file naming conventions
         - Including catalog-info.yaml in the template
         - .github/workflows for CI/CD generation
         [TEMPLATE SKELETON — Directory Structure]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  templates/django-service/                                                │
         │  |-- template.yaml              <- the Template entity itself             │
         │  |-- skeleton/                  <- copied + placeholder-replaced by       │
         │      |-- catalog-info.yaml       <- fetch:template                        │
         │      |     (name: ${{ values.name }})                                     │
         │      |-- Dockerfile                                                       │
         │      |-- requirements.txt                                                 │
         │      |-- manage.py                                                        │
         │      |-- .github/workflows/ci.yml                                         │
         │      |-- k8s/                                                             │
         │          |-- deployment.yaml                                              │
         │          |-- service.yaml                                                 │
         │                                                                           │
         │  RULE: every file inside skeleton/ can use {{ values.X }}                 │
         │  placeholders — fetch:template replaces them with the user's form input.  │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.3 Custom Template Actions
         - Creating a custom action with createTemplateAction
         - Input schema and validation
         - Output schema
         - Error handling
         [CUSTOM TEMPLATE ACTION — Real Code]
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  // plugins/scaffolder-backend-module-easypj/src/actions/notifySlack.ts     │
         │  import { createTemplateAction } from '@backstage/plugin-scaffolder-node';  │
         │                                                                             │
         │  export const createNotifySlackAction = () =>                               │
         │    createTemplateAction<{ channel: string; message: string }>({             │
         │      id: 'easypj:notify-slack',                                             │
         │      schema: {                                                              │
         │        input: {                                                             │
         │          required: ['channel', 'message'],                                  │
         │          properties: {                                                      │
         │            channel: { type: 'string' },                                     │
         │            message: { type: 'string' },                                     │
         │          },                                                                 │
         │        },                                                                   │
         │      },                                                                     │
         │      async handler(ctx) {                                                   │
         │        ctx.logger.info('Notifying channel: ' + ctx.input.channel);          │
         │        // call your Slack webhook here                                      │
         │        ctx.output('notified', true);                                        │
         │      },                                                                     │
         │    });                                                                      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Deciding whether you even need a custom action
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  IF YOU NEED TO...                              REACH FOR...                           │
         │  ----------------------------------------------  --------------------------------      │
         │  Copy files, replace {{ values.X }} placeholders   Built-in fetch:template (12.1)      │
         │  Create a repo on GitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket/Azure     Built-in publish:* (12.1)          │
         │  Register the result in the Catalog                  Built-in catalog:register (12.1)  │
         │  Notify a channel, call an internal API,               Write a custom action — no      │
         │   ping a ticketing system, run a org-specific check     built-in covers this (12.3)    │
         │                                                                                        │
         │  RULE OF THUMB: reach for a custom action only when the built-in catalog               │
         │  (12.1) has no action for what you need — most templates need zero custom code.        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    12.4 Template Testing & Debugging
         - Running templates locally
         - Debug mode: yarn dev with template debugging
         - Common template errors and fixes
         - Testing template output
         [TEMPLATE DEBUGGING — Commands and Common Errors]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # Validate template.yaml syntax before running it                         │
         │  yarn backstage-cli repo schema openapi validate ./template.yaml           │
         │                                                                            │
         │  # Run locally with debug logging                                          │
         │  BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug yarn dev                                        │
         │                                                                            │
         │  # Common errors:                                                          │
         │  'Input required property owner' -> a parameter marked required: [owner]   │
         │                                     wasn't filled in the form              │
         │  'Template not found'             -> wrong templateRef, or not registered  │
         │                                     in catalog.locations (with rules:      │
         │                                     [{allow: [Template]}])                 │
         │  Placeholder left unreplaced       -> typo in {{ values.X }},              │
         │  (literal {{ }} visible in          double-check the parameter's exact     │
         │  the output)                         property name                         │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
    Every action registered with the scaffolder backend is, under the hood,
    just a function that reads ctx.input, does its work, and calls
    ctx.output(name, value) to publish results. The scaffolder engine strings
    these functions together in the exact order listed under spec.steps,
    passing a shared workspace directory between them — the same temp
    workspace fetch:template writes into and publish:github later reads from.
    Because every action shares this one interface, a custom action like
    easypj:notify-slack (12.3) can sit in the same steps: list as a built-in
    action like publish:github with zero special-casing by the engine.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
     🔑 The built-in action catalog covers fetching (fetch:template,
        fetch:plain, fetch:cookiecutter), publishing (publish:github/gitlab/
        bitbucket/azure), and catalog registration (catalog:register,
        catalog:write), plus small file utilities (fs:delete,
        roadiehq:utils:fs:replace).
     🔑 Every action's OUTPUT is addressable by later steps as
        ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }} — the wiring that lets register
        reach back to what publish produced without either action
        referencing the other by name.
     🔑 The skeleton/ directory is what fetch:template actually copies; every
        file inside it can use {{ values.X }} placeholders replaced with the
        user's form input.
     🔑 spec.output.links/text is what renders on the scaffolder Task
        completion page — skip it and the user sees only raw step logs, no
        clickable "Open in Catalog" link.
     🔑 A custom action is built with createTemplateAction, declares an input
        schema, and calls ctx.output(...) to hand results to later steps —
        the same contract as any built-in action.
     🔑 Rule of thumb: reach for a custom action only when the built-in
        catalog has no action for the job — most templates need zero custom
        code.
     🔑 The 3 most common template failures: a missing required parameter, a
        wrong/unregistered templateRef, or a typo'd {{ values.X }}
        placeholder left literally in the output.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
     ⚠️ Confusing catalog:write with catalog:register — catalog:write only
        writes a YAML file to disk, it does NOT register anything in the
        Catalog.
     ⚠️ Assuming a custom action needs to reinvent file-copying — most custom
        actions should call an external system (Slack, ticketing, a check)
        and let the built-ins handle files/repos/catalog.
     ⚠️ Not marking a custom action's input properties as `required` — an
        unvalidated ctx.input.X can be undefined at runtime and crash the
        handler.
     ⚠️ Missing rules: [{allow: [Template]}] on the catalog.locations entry
        that points at a template — a template not marked as such won't run.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
     ⚡ Validate template.yaml syntax before running it (see cheat-card)
        instead of debugging blind inside the Create wizard.
     ⚡ Run with BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug when a step's output doesn't seem
        to flow into the next step — the debug log shows the resolved
        {{ steps.* }} values.
     ⚡ Check the "IF YOU NEED TO... REACH FOR..." decision table (12.3)
        before writing any custom action.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 12 — COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER                                    │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Validate template.yaml syntax before running it                           │
         │  $ yarn backstage-cli repo schema openapi validate ./template.yaml           │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Run locally with debug logging (see resolved steps.*.output values)       │
         │  $ BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug yarn dev                                        │
         │                                                                              │
         │  # Reading step output in a LATER step:                                      │
         │    ${{ steps.<id>.output.<field> }}                                          │
         │                                                                              │
         │  Common errors -> fix:                                                       │
         │    'Input required property owner'  -> required: [owner] not filled in form  │
         │    'Template not found'              -> not registered in catalog.locations  │
         │    literal {{ }} left in output      -> typo in the {{ values.X }} name      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🧪 PRACTICE DRILL (10 minutes)
     🧪 Write the createTemplateAction skeleton for a custom action with one
        required string input and one output field, without looking at 12.3.
     🧪 Pick one built-in action from the 12.1 reference table and state,
        from memory, its key input and its output.
     🧪 Time yourself: explain the difference between catalog:register and
        catalog:write in under 30 seconds.

    12.5 CBA Exam: Chapter 12 Practice Questions (10 Questions)
         - Q1-Q10: Actions, templates, debugging, custom actions

         [CHAPTER 12 VISUAL SUMMARY — How actions chain and where to look when stuck]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 12 VISUAL SUMMARY — How actions chain and where to look when stuck  │
         │                                                                              │
         │    🧩 built-in action catalog (12.1)      📁 skeleton/ directory (12.2)      │
         │       fetch:* / publish:* / catalog:*  ──▶  {{ values.X }} placeholders      │
         │                                                                              │
         │                     │  no built-in fits?                                     │
         │                     ▼                                                        │
         │         🛠️  custom action (12.3): createTemplateAction                      │
         │            ctx.input  ──▶  handler()  ──▶  ctx.output(...)                   │
         │                                                                              │
         │                     │                                                        │
         │                     ▼                                                        │
         │         🐞 test & debug (12.4): validate schema, BACKSTAGE_LOG_LEVEL=debug   │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         .vs-prose — In words: 12.1 gave the complete built-in action catalog —
         the tools that cover most templates. 12.2 showed the skeleton/
         directory those fetch actions actually copy, and how {{ values.X }}
         placeholders inside it get replaced. When neither is enough, 12.3
         showed how to write a custom action with the exact same ctx.input →
         handler → ctx.output(...) contract as any built-in. Finally 12.4
         closed the loop: validate the template's schema and run with debug
         logging to catch the 3 most common failures before they reach a user.


Chapter 13: Building Templates for easypj 🎯 CBA 32% (Customizing)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

    [META] 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~40min · CBA Domain: 32% (Customizing)

    WHY THIS MATTERS
    Chapters 11-12 taught the theory of Software Templates in the abstract.
    Chapter 13 makes it concrete: the exact 4 templates the easypj platform
    team maintains today — a Django microservice generator, a React frontend
    generator, a DB migration job generator, and a Terraform infrastructure
    generator — each a complete, copy-ready template.yaml you can register
    for easypj as-is.

    OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
    ✅ name all 4 easypj templates and what each one scaffolds (service,
       website, service, resource);
    ✅ read a complete production template.yaml end to end (metadata,
       parameters, steps) for each of the 4 templates;
    ✅ recognize the shared 3-step shape (fetch → publish:github →
       catalog:register) common to all 4 templates;
    ✅ identify what differs between templates (skeleton contents, type,
       owner) versus what stays identical (the step wiring);
    ✅ register all 4 templates into the Catalog via catalog.locations
       entries in app-config.yaml;
    ✅ explain how tags and categories surface recommended vs optional
       templates in the Create UI.

    CBA RELEVANCE: Chapter 13 is where the Customizing domain (🎯 32%) gets
    tested end-to-end — expect exam scenarios that hand you a broken or
    partial template.yaml matching one of these 4 patterns and ask you to
    complete or fix it.

    SECTION MAP (you are here):
    13.1 Template 1: Django Microservice → 13.2 Template 2: React Frontend →
    13.3 Template 3: Database Migration Job → 13.4 Template 4: Infrastructure
    as Code → 13.5 Registering Templates in the Catalog → 13.6 CBA Exam:
    Chapter 13 Practice Questions.

         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  THE 4 easypj TEMPLATES — AT A GLANCE                                                   │
         │                                                                                         │
         │  TEMPLATE                  TYPE      OWNER          SCAFFOLDS                           │
         │  -------------------------  --------  -------------  --------------------------------   │
         │  django-service (13.1)       service   team-platform   New Django backend microservice  │
         │  react-frontend (13.2)        website   team-fe          New React frontend app         │
         │  db-migration-job (13.3)       service   team-platform     K8s Job for a one-off DB     │
         │                                                             migration                   │
         │  infra-as-code (13.4)           resource  team-platform      Terraform for new cloud    │
         │                                                               infrastructure            │
         │                                                                                         │
         │  All 4 share the same 3-step shape: fetch -> publish:github -> catalog:register         │
         │  (see the 12.1 execution-flow diagram) - only the skeleton/ contents differ.            │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.1 Template 1: New Django Microservice
         - Purpose: Scaffold a new Django app for the easypj ecosystem
         - template.yaml: Complete YAML
         - Skeleton: Django boilerplate with best practices
         - Parameters: Service name, owner, description, Python version
         - Output: GitHub repo + registered in Catalog
         [TEMPLATE 1 — Django Microservice — Complete template.yaml]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: scaffolder.backstage.io/v1beta3                                     │
         │  kind: Template                                                                  │
         │  metadata:                                                                       │
         │    name: django-service                                                          │
         │    title: New Django Microservice                                                │
         │    tags: [django, python, recommended]                                           │
         │  spec:                                                                           │
         │    owner: team-platform                                                          │
         │    type: service                                                                 │
         │    parameters:                                                                   │
         │      - title: Service Details                                                    │
         │        required: [name, owner]                                                   │
         │        properties:                                                               │
         │          name: { type: string, pattern: "^[a-z0-9-]+$" }                         │
         │          owner: { type: string, ui:field: OwnerPicker }                          │
         │          pythonVersion: { type: string, enum: ['3.11','3.12'] }                  │
         │    steps:                                                                        │
         │      - id: fetch                                                                 │
         │        action: fetch:template                                                    │
         │        input: { url: ./skeleton, values: { name: ${{ parameters.name }} } }      │
         │      - id: publish                                                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                                    │
         │        input: { repoUrl: "github.com?owner=easypj&repo=${{ parameters.name }}" } │
         │      - id: register                                                              │
         │        action: catalog:register                                                  │
         │        input: { repoContentsUrl: "${{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}" } │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.2 Template 2: New React Frontend
         - Purpose: Scaffold a new React frontend app

         - Skeleton: Create React App boilerplate

         │    owner: team-fe                                                                │
         │    type: website                                                                 │
         │    parameters:                                                                   │
         │      - title: App Details                                                        │
         │        required: [name, owner]                                                   │
         │        properties:                                                               │
         │          name: { type: string, pattern: "^[a-z0-9-]+$" }                         │
         │          owner: { type: string, ui:field: OwnerPicker }                          │
         │    steps:                                                                        │
         │      - id: fetch                                                                 │
         │        action: fetch:template                                                    │
         │        input: { url: ./skeleton, values: { name: {{ parameters.name }} } }       │
         │      - id: publish                                                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                                    │
         │        input: { repoUrl: "github.com?owner=easypj&repo={{ parameters.name }}" }  │
         │      - id: register                                                              │
         │        action: catalog:register                                                  │
         │        input: { repoContentsUrl: "{{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}" }  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         │          name: { type: string }                                                  │
         │          targetService: { type: string, ui:field: EntityPicker }                 │
         │          isCronJob: { type: boolean, default: false }                            │
         │    steps:                                                                        │
         │      - id: fetch                                                                 │
         │        action: fetch:template                                                    │

         │      - id: publish                                                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                                    │

         │      - id: register                                                              │

    13.4 Template 4: Infrastructure as Code

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

         │    name: infra-as-code                                                           │
         │    title: New Terraform Infrastructure                                           │
         │    tags: [terraform, infrastructure]                                             │
         │  spec:                                                                           │
         │    owner: team-platform                                                          │
         │    type: resource                                                                │
         │    parameters:                                                                   │
         │      - title: Infrastructure Details                                             │
         │        required: [name, cloudProvider]                                           │
         │        properties:                                                               │
         │          name: { type: string }                                                  │
         │          cloudProvider: { type: string, enum: [aws, gcp, azure] }                │
         │    steps:                                                                        │
         │      - id: fetch                                                                 │
         │        action: fetch:template                                                    │
         │        input: { url: ./skeleton }                                                │
         │        # skeleton contains: main.tf, variables.tf, outputs.tf, backend.tf        │
         │      - id: publish                                                               │
         │        action: publish:github                                                    │
         │        input: { repoUrl: "github.com?owner=easypj&repo={{ parameters.name }}" }  │
         │      - id: register                                                              │
         │        action: catalog:register                                                  │
         │        input: { repoContentsUrl: "{{ steps.publish.output.repoContentsUrl }}" }  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.5 Registering Templates in the Catalog
         - How templates appear in Backstage UI
         - Template categories and tags
         - Recommended vs optional templates
         - Template lifecycle management
         [REGISTERING ALL 4 TEMPLATES — app-config.yaml]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  catalog:                                                  │
         │    locations:                                              │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/django-service/template.yaml    │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/react-frontend/template.yaml    │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/db-migration-job/template.yaml  │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         │      - type: file                                          │
         │        target: ./templates/infra-as-code/template.yaml     │
         │        rules:                                              │
         │          - allow: [Template]                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD
    All 4 easypj templates are registered the same way every other Template
    entity is: a type: file catalog.locations entry pointing at each
    template.yaml, gated by rules: [{allow: [Template]}] (13.5). Under the
    hood this means the Catalog's ingestion pipeline (Part 3, 7.2) treats a
    Template exactly like it treats a Component or a System — an entity
    provider reads the location, a processor validates and stores it, and the
    template becomes queryable/searchable like any other catalog entity. That
    is also why a broken template.yaml (bad YAML, missing required fields)
    fails the same way a broken catalog-info.yaml would: it simply won't
    appear in the Catalog until the syntax is fixed.

    🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
     🔑 easypj maintains exactly 4 templates today: django-service (service),
        react-frontend (website), db-migration-job (service), infra-as-code
        (resource).
     🔑 All 4 share the identical 3-step shape: fetch → publish:github →
        catalog:register — only the skeleton/ contents and parameters differ.
     🔑 django-service and db-migration-job are both owned by team-platform
        and typed service, despite scaffolding very different things (a
        Django app versus a one-off K8s Job).
     🔑 react-frontend is the only one of the 4 typed website and owned by
        team-fe.
     🔑 infra-as-code is the only one typed resource — it scaffolds Terraform
        (main.tf/variables.tf/outputs.tf/backend.tf), not application code.
     🔑 All 4 templates are registered as type: file catalog.locations
        entries in app-config.yaml, each with rules: [{allow: [Template]}].
     🔑 Templates, like every catalog entity, become visible in the
        Backstage Create wizard as soon as their location is registered — no
        extra deploy step beyond a config reload.

    ⚠️ EXAM GOTCHAS
     ⚠️ Assuming every template must be type: service — infra-as-code is
        type: resource and react-frontend is type: website; spec.type is a
        free classification, not fixed to the action steps.
     ⚠️ Copy-pasting one template's owner (e.g. team-platform) into another
        without checking who actually owns it (react-frontend is team-fe).
     ⚠️ Forgetting the rules: [{allow: [Template]}] restriction on each
        catalog.locations entry — without it Backstage may misinterpret the
        file as a different kind.
     ⚠️ Missing a required parameter (e.g. targetService on db-migration-job,
        cloudProvider on infra-as-code) so the generated skeleton has no way
        to know what it's targeting.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
     ⚡ Start any new easypj template from whichever of the 4 existing ones
        is structurally closest (same type/owner) and only change the
        skeleton/ and parameters.
     ⚡ Keep the fetch → publish:github → catalog:register step IDs and shape
        identical across templates — consistency here makes slotting in a
        custom action (12.3) easy without re-learning the wiring.
     ⚡ Register a new template with a single extra catalog.locations entry —
        no code change to Backstage itself is required.

    🔑 COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CHAPTER 13 — COMMANDS/CONFIG TO REMEMBER                                │
         │                                                                          │
         │  THE 4 easypj TEMPLATES (name / type / owner):                           │
         │    django-service     service   team-platform                            │
         │    react-frontend     website   team-fe                                  │
         │    db-migration-job   service   team-platform                            │
         │    infra-as-code      resource  team-platform                            │
         │                                                                          │
         │  # Generic registration pattern (app-config.yaml), repeat per template:  │
         │  catalog:                                                                │
         │    locations:                                                            │
         │      - type: file                                                        │
         │        target: ./templates/<name>/template.yaml                          │
         │        rules:                                                            │
         │          - allow: [Template]                                             │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🧪 PRACTICE DRILL (10 minutes)
     🧪 Without looking back: list the 4 easypj templates, their spec.type,
        and their owner.
     🧪 Write the catalog.locations entry needed to register a brand-new
        templates/notification-service/template.yaml.
     🧪 Pick one of the 4 templates and name the one parameter that is
        unique to it (not shared by the others).
